# SHE STOLE MY FACE — EP43

## S#1. Recovery Facility — Mara's Suite · Morning

[VISUAL/ACTION]
A quiet recovery facility upstate — a converted manor house turned residential rehabilitation, soft beige walls, real wood floors, a window with actual trees outside it instead of city. MARA's suite is small, with a single bed already made, a writing table by the window, and a soft armchair angled toward the trees. Mara is in plain grey sweats and a black long-sleeve, her hair tied back severely, no jewelry, the faint surgical scars at her jaw and inner wrist no longer hidden. A tray with breakfast sits half-eaten on the side table. There is a knock. A young woman in facility uniform brings in a single envelope on a small wooden tray.

[KEY CAMERA]
- Wide of the suite, the window light on the wood floor
- MCU Mara at the writing table
- OTS the staff member at the door with the tray
- ECU the envelope — no return address, only Mara's first name in old-fashioned handwriting

[DIALOGUE]
STAFF MEMBER
This came through the front desk for you this morning. They said a courier dropped it. We checked it through intake. There's nothing inside it but a letter. We thought you'd want to see it before we filed it.

MARA
(taking the tray)
Thank you. You can leave the door open.

The staff member nods and goes. Mara sets the tray on the writing table. She does not open the envelope immediately. She looks at it for a long beat. Then she lifts a small letter opener from the desk and slits the top.

[VO]
MARA (V.O.)
The handwriting on the front of the envelope was my mother's. She had been in custody for nine days. There were five people on the planet who knew this address and none of them worked at her facility. Somebody had carried it out for her. Somebody she still had a hand on.


## S#2. Mara's Suite · The Letter

[VISUAL/ACTION]
Mara unfolds the letter. Two pages, both sides, the same cramped neat hand as everything else of Eileen's. She reads it once through, sitting very still. She reaches the end. She lays it flat on the desk. She does not crumple it. She does not tear it. She picks up her phone from the desk and sets it on the desk in front of her, screen up. She opens the camera. She turns it to face her.

[KEY CAMERA]
- MCU Mara at the desk, the letter beside her, the phone propped up
- ECU the phone screen, the front camera framing her face
- OTS Mara → the camera
- INSERT: the letter, the first line in Eileen's handwriting

[DIALOGUE]
MARA
(quiet, level, to the camera, no theater)
My name is Mara Voss. I'm in a recovery facility upstate. I'm not supposed to have a phone in my room but I've been allowed one for medical reasons and I'm going to use it for this. This morning I received a letter from my mother. She is in custody. She is not supposed to be able to reach me. She reached me anyway. I'm going to read it to you. I'm going to stop after each paragraph and I'm going to tell you what she's doing in it. I think that's the right thing to do with it.

(she lifts the letter)

She begins with: "My dearest Mara. I have been thinking about you every night since they took me from the airfield." That's the first sentence. It's the line where she tells me she's been thinking about me every night. I'd like you to notice that the very first sentence of this letter is a piece of bait. She knows the only way I'll keep reading is if I believe the woman who tried to put me in an elevator has been thinking about me every night. So she puts that there first. It's the same opening she's used in every letter she's ever written me, and I've checked.

(beat)

The next paragraph is: "I know the people around you are telling you a version of me that I don't recognize. They are not interested in our story. Only we can know what we were to each other." That's the line where she tells me only she has ever loved me. It's the line where she tells me everyone around me is trying to take her place. I'd like you to notice that she does not name a single person. She just calls them "they." That's how this works. When the people are vague, the loyalty stays specific.

(she turns the page)

Here's the line I want to spend the most time on. "Mara. There is nowhere else for you. You and I both know it. The world has a use for the woman they think you were. It has none for the woman you actually are. Come home." That's the line where she tells me I have nowhere else to go. It's the line where she tells me the woman I actually am isn't worth anything to anybody but her. I'd like you to notice that she has spent fifteen years making sure I believed that. I'd like you to notice she is still trying. And I'd like you to notice — because I'd like to notice it out loud, so I don't forget I noticed it — that she's wrong. I have somewhere else. I'm sitting in it. The food on the tray behind me was brought to me by a person who said good morning to me by my real name. That isn't a small thing.

(she lays the letter back down on the desk)

I'm going to stop the video here. I'm going to upload it. I'm not asking anyone to do anything with it. I'm just putting it where she can't take it back.

[KEY CAMERA]
- MCU Mara reaching out and tapping the screen
- ECU the upload prompt
- CU her finger pressing PUBLISH
- OTS Mara → the trees outside the window


## S#3. Recovery Facility — Front Garden · Afternoon

[VISUAL/ACTION]
A few hours later. The front garden of the facility, a gravel path running between low hedges. Mara is in a dark coat over the grey sweats, her hair still tied back, walking slowly down the path on her own for the first time since she was admitted. A staff nurse watches from the front steps but does not follow. Mara reaches the gate at the end of the path. She stops there. She does not go through. She turns around and walks back toward the house.

[KEY CAMERA]
- Wide of the garden, Mara walking the path
- MCU Mara at the gate
- ECU her hand on the gate latch, then lifting away
- OTS Mara → the front of the house

[VO]
MARA (V.O.)
I had thought before I went outside that I might walk through the gate. I had wanted to know if I could. When I got there I found that I didn't need to. The gate was just a gate. It would still be there tomorrow. I could decide tomorrow.

[INSERT/CUTAWAY]
A single phone notification flashing across a feed on a passerby's screen in the city, miles away — Mara's face, the headline "Daughter Reads Mother's Letter Aloud." The view count is climbing. Across the screen, a comment scrolls: "She named every move. Every single one." Another: "She's going to be okay."


## S#4. Noah's Apartment — Living Room · Evening

[VISUAL/ACTION]
Noah's apartment, soft warm light from the standing lamp in the corner, the city visible through the window but not the focus. NOAH sits at one end of the couch in a soft grey henley, sleeves pushed to the elbow, his feet bare on the rug. LENA is along the length of the couch, her head on his shoulder, one of his hands resting on her wrist. A film is playing on the television across the room, the sound low — something old, black and white, neither of them looking at it. On the coffee table, two plates and two glasses and the last of a takeout meal.

[KEY CAMERA]
- Wide of the couch from across the room
- MCU two-shot, Lena against his shoulder, his head turned slightly down
- ECU his hand on her wrist, very gentle weight
- CU Lena's eyes, half-closed, on the TV she is not watching

[DIALOGUE]
NOAH
(quiet)
I saw the video this morning. I watched it twice. The second time I watched it for the parts where she stops and breathes.

LENA
She did that on purpose. She told me on the phone last week that she was practicing. Breathing low and slow, between sentences, until it stops feeling like she has to perform the calm.

NOAH
She's doing it. She's actually doing it. I wasn't sure she would.

LENA
Neither was I. I'm glad I was wrong.

(beat, settling deeper against him)

We haven't talked about anything except the case for two days. I think tonight we're going to keep not talking about it. Is that all right.

NOAH
(small smile, looking down at her)
It's more than all right.

LENA
What did you do today that wasn't this.

NOAH
I went to a barber. The barber asked me how my week was and I said good for the first time in three months and meant it. Then I bought a loaf of bread on the way home. I don't even like bread.

LENA
You don't like bread.

NOAH
I do not. I bought it anyway.

LENA
That's a very strange thing to do.

NOAH
It was a very strange morning.


## S#5. Noah's Apartment — Living Room · Continuous

[VISUAL/ACTION]
The film on the television cuts to a song the two of them apparently know, because Noah hums one bar of it under his breath and Lena, without lifting her head from his shoulder, lets out the smallest laugh.

[KEY CAMERA]
- MCU two-shot, both faces visible
- ECU his thumb moving once along her wrist
- CU the TV reflected in the window glass

[DIALOGUE]
LENA
(very quiet, not lifting her head)
I love you.

NOAH
(after a beat, his head turning very slightly so his mouth is closer to her hair)
I love you. I have been waiting to say that to you when nothing was on fire.

LENA
Nothing is on fire.

NOAH
I noticed.

She doesn't move. He doesn't move. The film keeps playing. Outside, the city goes about its evening. Neither of them looks at the phone on the coffee table.

[VO]
LENA (V.O.)
Three years ago a man told me I was the woman he loved at his own engagement party and I believed him for forty minutes before the rest of the night unmade it. I had been afraid of those four words for so long after that. I had thought I would say them again the way you cross a bridge in the dark — fast, holding your breath, hoping the other side existed. I didn't cross any bridge tonight. We were already on the other side. I'd just stopped pretending we weren't.


## S#6. Lena's Phone · Late

[VISUAL/ACTION]
The phone on the coffee table buzzes once. The screen lights. ETHAN. A single text preview is visible on the lock screen. Lena lifts her head from Noah's shoulder, picks up the phone, reads it without unlocking. She reads it once. She sets the phone face-down on the coffee table. She settles back against Noah.

[KEY CAMERA]
- ECU the phone screen, the message visible — "Saw Mara's video. She did a brave thing. Your turn next."
- MCU Lena reading
- CU her thumb on the screen, not unlocking
- OTS Lena → Noah's shoulder as she settles back

[DIALOGUE]
NOAH
(without lifting his head, eyes still half on the film)
Whatever it is, it can wait until tomorrow.

LENA
It can wait.

[END HOOK]
Hold on the phone face-down on the coffee table. Hold on Lena's hand returning to where it had been on Noah's chest. Hold on the lamp, the only light in the room, steady. Outside the window the city keeps going. Inside the apartment nobody moves.

Hard Cut.

# SHE STOLE MY FACE — EP44

## S#1. Holding Facility — Cell Corridor · Morning

[VISUAL/ACTION]
A narrow concrete corridor in the holding facility where EILEEN has been remanded the past six weeks. A uniformed guard walks her back from the visiting room toward her cell — the cell where we last saw her writing the letter in EP42. Eileen is in pale institutional blue, the silver-grey wool coat long gone, her hair pulled back into a flat plain bun. Her hands are loosely at her sides, unshackled inside the building. She does not look at the guard or at the corridor camera. The guard stops her at the cell door, holds it open. She steps inside without being asked twice.

[KEY CAMERA]
- Wide of the concrete corridor, the guard half a step behind
- MCU Eileen walking, head level
- ECU the cell door swinging shut behind her
- INSERT: a small wall-mounted clock at the corridor turn, the second hand moving

[VO]
EILEEN (V.O.)
The transfer request had been denied that morning. I had been told in two sentences, in a room with no window. There would be no chronic-condition relocation, no separate wing, no quieter place for me to wait out the rest of my life. I would stay where I was. I had spent sixteen years telling my daughter that the worst thing a woman could be was small. I had not yet found a word for what I was in this room. I would find one in time.

[VISUAL/ACTION]
She crosses to the bunk and sits on the edge of it. She does not lie down. She folds her hands in her lap. She looks at the small high window — barred, two feet square — for a long count without moving.

[KEY CAMERA]
- ECU Eileen's hands folded in her lap
- CU her eyes, no movement, no narrowing
- Wide of the cell from the door — Eileen at the bunk, the small high window above her
- Long lens through the cell-door slot, holding


## S#2. Recovery Facility — Mara's Suite · Same Morning

[VISUAL/ACTION]
INTERCUT — Mara's suite at the recovery facility, the writing table by the window. MARA is in a soft camel coat over plain dark clothes, hair tied back loosely now rather than severely. Her single suitcase stands by the door, already zipped. On the table: a slim travel folder, her passport sitting on top of it — MARA VOSS visible on the data page where she has set it open to it on purpose. No documents around it. Just the passport, alone on the wood. The young woman from earlier — the staff member, now in a coat as if she will be going off shift — leans in the doorway briefly.

[KEY CAMERA]
- MCU Mara at the desk, the passport in front of her
- ECU the passport — MARA VOSS visible
- INSERT: the single zipped suitcase by the door
- OTS the staff member at the door

[DIALOGUE]
STAFF MEMBER
Your car is at the gate when you're ready. The driver said no rush. He'll wait as long as you need.

MARA
Thank you. I'll be down in ten minutes.

She closes the passport. She tucks it into the inside pocket of the camel coat. She looks once around the room — at the bed, at the armchair, at the trees outside the window — and lifts the suitcase by the handle. Whatever paperwork had brought her here is finished. She is the only thing left in the room to move.

[VO]
MARA (V.O.)
The court had asked me where I wanted to go. I had told them I didn't know yet. They had let me leave anyway, on the conditions Lena and I had quietly negotiated through Bannon — no interviews, no books, no profile pieces, even if anyone asked. I would be quiet for the rest of my life about what had happened. That was the deal I had wanted. That was the deal I had got.


## S#3. Airport — International Terminal · Late Afternoon

[VISUAL/ACTION]
A mid-sized international airport, the wide quiet hall of the long-haul terminal in the early-afternoon lull between bank flights. Polished floor, the long row of windows, the planes visible at the gates. MARA walks beside LENA across the concourse, the single suitcase rolling behind her, the camel coat over a plain dark sweater. Lena is in a slate-grey wool coat, no necklace today — the necklace is in her bag — her hair down. NOAH is a tactful ten paces behind them, hands in his coat pockets, giving them the floor.

Just past the second set of windows, Mara slows. There is a newsstand kiosk at the corner of the concourse — a small wall of magazines facing out. The cover of one of them, a national weekly, carries a black-and-white close-up of EILEEN VOSS at the airfield in the silver-grey coat from EP41, the cuffs visible at her wrists. The headline runs in heavy red type across the bottom: **MOTHER ARRESTED — THE WOMAN WHO PAID FOR HER DAUGHTER'S FACE**. Underneath in smaller print: *Special report — 12 pages.*

Mara stops in front of the cover. She doesn't pick it up. Two passing travelers slow without meaning to, look from the magazine to her face, recognize one shape inside the other, look away. A teenage girl behind them notices second, lifts her phone halfway, sees Mara's eyes on her, lowers the phone again. No one says anything. The kiosk attendant is reading her own phone behind the counter and has not looked up at any of it.

[KEY CAMERA]
- MCU Mara stopped in front of the cover
- ECU the magazine cover — Eileen's face, the headline
- CU the two passing travelers' eyes flicking from the cover to Mara
- ECU the teenage girl's phone lifting halfway, then lowering

[VISUAL/ACTION]
Mara does not face card the strangers down. She does not perform anything. She lets the moment of being seen be a moment of being smaller in public. She turns her face away from the kiosk first, and then her body. Lena waits a half-step behind her, not touching, not steering. The travelers move on. Mara starts walking again. The suitcase wheels roll once more.

[VO]
MARA (V.O.)
The first thing I had learned in the last six weeks was that I would never get to be invisible again. I was the woman in the kiosk wall, in print, for an afternoon. I was going to have to learn how to stand inside that and keep walking. I started today.

[VISUAL/ACTION]
They reach a quiet bench near the security entrance and stop.

[KEY CAMERA]
- Wide of the terminal, the two women crossing the floor
- MCU two-shot, Lena and Mara walking
- ECU the suitcase wheels on the polished floor
- OTS Lena → Mara as they reach the bench

[DIALOGUE]
LENA
You don't have to go through right away. You can stand here for another twenty minutes. I'm not in a hurry.

MARA
I'd rather go through. I'd rather not stand here being seen off.

LENA
All right. Then let's say what we're going to say standing here for two minutes instead of twenty.

MARA
That's the right amount of time for what I want to say to you.


## S#4. Airport — Before Security · Continuous

[VISUAL/ACTION]
They stop beside the bench but neither of them sits. Mara sets the suitcase down by her feet, hands loose at her sides. The afternoon light through the long windows is the soft white of a winter afternoon, and it lays across both their faces evenly. No hug. No camera click. Just the two of them in front of the gate.

[KEY CAMERA]
- Two-shot from the side, the long windows behind them
- MCU Mara, the faint scar under her jaw visible in the flat light
- MCU Lena, listening
- ECU her hands at her sides, deliberately loose

[DIALOGUE]
MARA
You could have let her finish me. In that elevator. You knew where I was for at least three minutes before you started the call to your sergeant. Noah told me. He said you had three minutes to make a different call. You didn't.

LENA
She doesn't get to decide who lives. Neither do I. But I can decide who I show up for. I decided you. I'd decide you again.

MARA
(after a long beat, looking down at the polished floor between them, then back up)
I'm not going to thank you for that. I don't think you'd want me to. I think you'd rather I went and lived somewhere that didn't have your name on a building, and learned how to sit in a room and read for an afternoon without thinking about who I used to want to be.

LENA
That's what I'd rather.

MARA
Then that's what I'll do.

(beat)

There's one thing I want to ask you to do for me. I'm not going to write. I'd like you not to write either. Once I'm settled — once I have a kitchen and a chair and a name on a lease somewhere — I'll send you a postcard. One. After that, you'll know I'm fine. After that, I'd like us to leave it.

LENA
A postcard. I can do a postcard.

[VO]
LENA (V.O.)
I had thought for weeks about what I would say to her at this gate. I had practiced sentences and cut them down twice. When I got here none of the practiced sentences had anything to do with what either of us actually needed. We needed two minutes and a bench we did not sit on. The rest had said itself.


## S#5. Airport — Security Gate · Continuous

[VISUAL/ACTION]
Mara picks up her suitcase by the handle. She turns toward the security entrance. She walks the short distance to the rope barrier. She hands her passport and boarding pass to the agent at the lane. Lena stays at the bench. Noah, behind, has stopped at a respectful distance and is watching the gate, not Lena.

[KEY CAMERA]
- MCU Mara at the rope barrier, suitcase in one hand
- ECU her passport handed across the counter
- CU Lena watching from the bench
- OTS Mara → the security lane ahead of her

[DIALOGUE]
MARA
(turning back at the rope barrier, half a step, voice carrying just to Lena)
I'll send you a postcard. From the woman I should have been.

LENA
(steady)
Send it from the woman you're going to be.

MARA
(small, almost a smile)
That too.

She turns. She walks through the rope barrier. She does not look back again. The agent waves her through the metal detector. She steps through. She collects her suitcase from the X-ray belt. She walks down the long corridor toward her gate. Lena watches her until she is past the glass partition. Then she watches the empty corridor for another count.

[KEY CAMERA]
- Wide of the security corridor, Mara walking away from camera
- MCU Lena at the bench, very still
- ECU her hand on the strap of her bag, not tightening
- Long lens down the corridor, Mara becoming smaller


## S#6. Airport — Concourse · Five Minutes Later

[VISUAL/ACTION]
Lena turns from the bench. Noah is already crossing the concourse to meet her. They do not touch. He falls in beside her and they walk together back toward the parking exit, the long windows on their right, the planes on the tarmac sliding past in the periphery.

[KEY CAMERA]
- Two-shot from behind, Lena and Noah walking the concourse
- MCU Lena, the long light on her face
- ECU her phone in her hand, dim
- Wide of the polished floor, the two figures crossing it

[DIALOGUE]
NOAH
(at her elbow, quiet)
You all right.

LENA
I'm all right. I'd like to be home before the light goes.

NOAH
Let's go.

[END HOOK]
Lena's phone buzzes once in her hand. She looks down. NOAH, single text, sent eight minutes ago, before he walked back over to her: "Come home." She reads it. She doesn't reply. She slides the phone into her coat pocket. Hold on the wide shot of the two of them crossing the polished floor of the concourse, the long windows on their right, the planes sliding past, the afternoon light. The terminal is quiet around them. Lena keeps walking. The doors at the end of the concourse part as she reaches them. She steps through. Hold on the closing doors. Hold on the empty bench behind her, the long light on the polished floor, the place where Mara had set her suitcase down for two minutes and then picked it up again.

# SHE STOLE MY FACE — EP45

## S#1. Cross Manor — Front Drive · Mid-morning

[VISUAL/ACTION]
The front drive of Cross Manor — the same house Lena hasn't crossed the threshold of since the morning after the cathedral. Tall plane trees, the gravel raked clean, no security. NOAH's car comes up the long drive at twenty miles an hour. There is no press at the gate. There is no press at the door. There is no announcement. This is a private morning. LENA is in slate-grey wool, her mother's necklace at her throat, hair half-up with the single pearl pin. Noah parks. He cuts the engine. He looks at her.

[KEY CAMERA]
- Wide of the manor drive, the car coming up
- MCU Lena in the passenger seat, looking at the front door
- OTS Noah → Lena
- ECU Lena's hand brushing the necklace once, then dropping

[DIALOGUE]
NOAH
You don't owe them this. You can tell me to back the car out right now and I will.

LENA
I know. I'd rather do it once and not have to do it again. Come in with me. I'd like you in the room.

NOAH
I'll be in the room.


## S#2. Cross Manor — Sitting Room · The Apology

[VISUAL/ACTION]
A quiet morning sitting room on the south side of the house — sun through tall windows, two facing couches, a low table between them with a tea service nobody has touched. No staff in the room. No camera. No phone visible. VICTORIA in dove grey, no pearl choker for once, just a thin gold chain. ETHAN in a charcoal suit, the engagement band gone from his hand for months, sitting two cushions from his mother. Lena sits on the opposite couch with Noah at her elbow. Four people in the room. The doors are closed.

[KEY CAMERA]
- Wide of the sitting room, four people on two couches
- MCU Victoria on the far couch
- OTS Victoria → Lena across the low table
- CU Ethan, jaw set, hands quiet on his knees

[DIALOGUE]
VICTORIA
Three years ago, on the night before my son's engagement, a young woman was nearly killed in a car my family arranged for her. While she was in a hospital bed, my household and I allowed another woman to take her name, her clothes, and her seat at our table. I'm sitting in this room because I won't let myself say that line in print without saying it to you in person first. We did that. I did that. I let it continue for months after I knew something was wrong. I'm not asking you to forgive me. I'm asking you to know that I know.

(beat)

I'd like to do one thing publicly with the family name. The Helena Sterling Foundation — your foundation, your mother's name — Cross Industries will fund it jointly with Sterling, no review board, no annual vote. I'll sign whatever paperwork you ask me to sign. The press release goes out when you say it goes out. Not before. Your mother believed that work was the only thing in this city worth her name. I should have remembered that earlier.

ETHAN
(low, to Lena, not performing it)
What she just said is true. I want to add one thing I owe you personally. When you came to our engagement party in a hospital coat and tried to tell me who you were, I sent you out. I was looking at the woman beside me and I was telling myself I knew her. I didn't. I'm not asking for anything back. I'm sitting here because the only person in that room that night who was telling me the truth was the one I had thrown out of it. I'm sorry, Lena. For the door, and for everything that came after it.


## S#3. Cross Manor — Sitting Room · Lena's Turn

[VISUAL/ACTION]
Lena does not stand. She does not perform the answer. She sets her teacup down on the saucer once, the small sound of porcelain on porcelain the only sound for a beat. Noah at her elbow does not move. She looks at Victoria first, then at Ethan.

[KEY CAMERA]
- MCU Lena on the couch
- CU her hand setting the teacup down
- OTS Lena → Victoria, then → Ethan
- CU Noah at her elbow, watching her

[DIALOGUE]
LENA
I accept what was just said. My mother used to tell me an apology is only worth the next thing you do after it. So we'll see what comes after. I'm going to spend the next year on the foundation. I'd like the family side of it kept out of the press unless I bring you in. I'll send word when I'm ready to announce it publicly — not from a lectern, just a single release through Bannon's office and yours together. That's the part I'd rather you hear before anyone else hears it.

(beat — quieter)

There is a woman somewhere in the Mediterranean right now who used to be called by my name. She has a postcard going out next week. I have no comment on her, ever. I'd appreciate the same from this house.

VICTORIA
(after a long count)
You'll have it.

ETHAN
You'll have it from me too.


## S#4. Cross Manor — Front Hall · Ten Minutes Later

[VISUAL/ACTION]
Lena and Noah cross the front hall back toward the door. Victoria walks them, one step behind Lena's left shoulder. Ethan stays in the sitting room. The front door is already half-open in the morning light.

[KEY CAMERA]

- MCU Noah a step behind
- ECU Victoria's hand, gold chain caught between her fingers
- Wide of the open front door, the drive beyond

[DIALOGUE]
VICTORIA
The foundation paperwork will be on your desk by tomorrow. Cross Industries board has signed. You have full discretion on the first three years.

LENA
Thank you. I'll read it tonight.

VICTORIA
Lena. I don't expect anything from this. I'd still like to be useful to you, if you ever decide there's a use.

LENA
You'll know if there is. Take care of yourself, Victoria.

She does not hug her. She does not embrace Ethan back in the sitting room. She steps through the front door with Noah at her elbow. The door closes behind them.


## S#5. Lena's Apartment Building — Front Steps · Late Afternoon

[VISUAL/ACTION]
Lena and Noah climb the four front steps of her building. Lena is halfway to the door when she sees it — propped against the brass mail slot, a single postcard, no envelope. Sun-bleached on one corner like it's been on a boat. She picks it up. The picture side is a small white town built into a cliff above blue water, no caption. She turns it over.

> I'm reading the ocean for the first time. The dog at the cafe is named Comma. I keep being surprised that nobody here knows my face. — M.

[DIALOGUE]
NOAH
First one?

LENA
First one.

NOAH
Are you going to write back?

LENA
Not yet. I want her to send another one first.

[END HOOK]
Lena slides the postcard into her coat pocket. She unlocks the door. Inside, on the small foyer table, her phone buzzes once with a news alert — the chyron from the press atrium, repeating. She doesn't pick it up. She walks past it, into the apartment. Hold on the closed door from the inside hallway. # SHE STOLE MY FACE — EP46

## S#1. Glenmoor Cemetery — Helena Sterling's Plot · Mid-morning

[VISUAL/ACTION]
Light cloud over a slope of low headstones, mid-spring grass just turning. LENA walks the gravel alone in slate-grey wool and her mother's necklace at her throat. She carries a small leather portfolio under one arm and a single white peony loose in her right hand. She does not bring flowers from a florist's box; the peony is from her own kitchen counter. NOAH's car waits at the cemetery gate, two hundred meters off, engine quiet. He stays inside it. He understands today is hers.

The headstone reads HELENA MARGARET STERLING and a pair of dates. The stone is clean — Lena has had it cleaned every season since EP22. Lena lowers herself to the grass on one knee, sets the peony at the base of the stone, and rests the leather portfolio across her thigh.

[KEY CAMERA]
- Wide of Lena alone on the slope, Noah's car small at the gate
- MCU Lena kneeling at the stone, peony placed
- ECU Lena's hand opening the portfolio clasp
- CU the necklace at her throat catching one small bar of sun

[DIALOGUE]
LENA
(low, to the stone)
Hi, Mom. I brought the last piece. I wanted to read it to you before I read it to anyone else.

[INSERT/CUTAWAY]
Inside the leather portfolio: a single folded sheet of cream paper, edges softened by handling. Helena's handwriting — slanted, real, no flourishes — visible at the top corner: *for Lena, when she's ready*.


## S#2. Glenmoor Cemetery — Helena's Plot · The Last Page

[VISUAL/ACTION]
Lena unfolds the page in her lap. She does not look up. She does not perform for the absent. She reads it the way you read a real letter — slightly under her breath, with the natural pauses of someone hearing her mother's voice in her own throat. The wind moves the peony's petals once. Lena's hand on the page is steady.

[KEY CAMERA]
- OTS Lena → the page in her lap
- ECU Helena's handwriting at the top of the sheet
- MCU Lena reading, eyes lowered
- CU her throat where the necklace sits, swallowing once between paragraphs

[VO — HELENA, warm contralto, no theatrical lift, no recitation cadence]
Lena, if you've found this part you've already found most of the rest, and I'm guessing it took longer than I wanted. There's something I never wrote down in the other pages because I was hoping I'd live long enough to tell you in person.

For about eleven years I sent money once a quarter to a woman named Eileen Voss, and to her daughter through her. I told myself it was a kindness and I told your father it was a charity I didn't want to discuss. It was neither. Your father had a child before he met me, and I knew it, and I let Eileen keep the girl quiet on a small stipend instead of asking her into our family. I never asked where the money went after it left my account. I never tried to meet the girl. I think I was afraid that meeting her would make me have to share you with her, and I wasn't generous enough then to do that without flinching.

I am sorry for that. I'm sorry to her, and I'm sorry to you, because whatever she becomes you'll be the one standing in the same room with her, not me. If she shows up in your life, please look at her once before you decide who she is. That's all I'm asking. Just once.

I hope you've fallen in love with someone who lets you finish your sentences. I hope you've stopped apologizing for the room you take up. And keep the foundation work going if you can — I built it for women I wouldn't get to meet, and I'd rather it outlast both of us than feel like an obligation. If it stops feeling like ours, drop it. I'd rather you be alive than dutiful.

I loved you well. I tried, anyway. I'll stop signing letters now.
— Mom

[VISUAL/ACTION]
Lena lowers the page. The wind moves the peony again. She doesn't cry; her eyes are wet without spilling. She folds the letter back into thirds along its old creases. She slides it into the portfolio and closes the clasp.


## S#3. Glenmoor Cemetery — Helena's Plot · After

[VISUAL/ACTION]
Lena sits back on her heels. She looks at the stone for a long beat. She does not deliver a speech. She speaks the way you'd speak to a person who is in the next room and will hear you anyway.

[KEY CAMERA]
- MCU Lena sitting back on her heels
- CU her hand resting flat on the grass beside the peony
- ECU the dates carved on the stone

[DIALOGUE]
LENA
I think I understand now. I think I forgive both of you — you and him. It took me a while to get here and I'm not going to pretend it was clean. I'll come back next month. I'll tell you what happens.

(beat — small, real, almost private)
The man I'm going to marry is sitting in the car at the gate. You'd have liked him. He doesn't talk over me.


## S#4. Glenmoor Cemetery — Helena's Plot · The Necklace

[VISUAL/ACTION]
Lena reaches up behind her own neck. She unclasps the necklace — slow, careful, the way her mother taught her when she was nine. She lays it across the top edge of the headstone for one held breath. The diamond catches the sun. Lena watches it sit there. Then she lifts it back and clasps it around her own throat again. She presses her palm flat against the carved name for three seconds, then stands.

[KEY CAMERA]
- ECU Lena's fingers at the clasp at the back of her neck
- CU the necklace lying across the top of the headstone
- ECU Lena's palm flat against HELENA MARGARET STERLING
- MCU Lena standing, brushing grass from her knee

[VO — LENA, low, internal, one sentence only]
I'm taking it with me. You already know what it says.


## S#5. Cemetery Gate → Lena's Apartment Door · Late Morning into Afternoon

[VISUAL/ACTION]
Lena walks the gravel back down the slope to the gate. Noah is leaning on the passenger door of the car with a brown paper bag in one hand and two cardboard coffee cups stacked in the other. He doesn't push off the car until he's sure she's actually coming. When she's close enough he holds out one of the cups.

[KEY CAMERA]
- Wide of Lena walking down the slope toward the car
- MCU Noah at the car, paper bag and coffee
- Two-shot Lena and Noah at the open car door
- CU Lena taking the coffee, her thumb brushing his

[DIALOGUE]
LENA
You brought lunch to a graveyard?

NOAH
I figured you'd be hungry after. There's a soup place by the river I've been meaning to take you to anyway. We can eat in the car if you'd rather not sit somewhere people will look at you.

LENA
The car. Then home. I want to take this coat off and not see anyone for an hour.

NOAH
Done.

[INTERCUT]
Lena's apartment — front door. Late afternoon. Lena turns the key. Before she pushes the door open she sees it: a single postcard taped to the door at eye height with a strip of brown paper tape. The picture side faces her — a stretch of grey-blue water, a low painted lighthouse, no caption. Postmark blurred but legibly overseas. Lena pulls the tape carefully so the paint won't lift. She turns the card over in the hallway light.

[INSERT/CUTAWAY]
MARA's handwriting, blue ballpoint, slightly clumsy, three lines:

> I'm reading the ocean for the first time. The water here is too cold to swim. I'll send you a real photo of where I'm staying soon. — M.

[END HOOK]
Lena leans her forehead against the closed door for one beat with the postcard pressed flat to the wood under her palm. She breathes out once — the long breath she's been holding since the cemetery gate. She slides the postcard into the leather portfolio with her mother's last page. Inside the apartment, Noah is already in the kitchen, lifting two soup containers out of the paper bag like the day has been ordinary. Lena watches him for three seconds from the hallway. She doesn't say anything. She walks in and sets the portfolio on the kitchen island and reaches for one of the spoons. Hold on the leather portfolio, closed, sitting between them on the counter.

Hard Cut.

# SHE STOLE MY FACE — EP47

## S#1. Sterling House — Father's Study · Morning

[VISUAL/ACTION]
The study at the back of Sterling House — Lena's father's room. Bookshelves to the ceiling, the tall narrow window onto the garden, the leather chair behind the desk angled exactly where William Sterling left it the morning he last sat in it, eleven years ago. Nobody has cleaned it. Nobody has moved a book. The room has been kept the way a museum keeps the place a person was last alive. LENA stops at the doorway in a midnight-blue blouse and slate trousers, hair half-up with the single pearl pin, her mother's necklace at her throat. She has not entered this room since the funeral. She is alone. The house is quiet around her.

[KEY CAMERA]
- Wide of the study from the doorway, the leather chair empty
- MCU Lena at the threshold, one hand on the doorframe
- ECU the leather chair, the worn place on the right arm where William rested his elbow
- CU her hand letting go of the doorframe

[VO]
LENA (V.O.)
I had been the head of my father's company in fact for six months and in name for two. He had died at fifty-one. I had not sat at his desk yet. I had told myself it was sentiment. It was something smaller than that. I had been afraid of the chair.

[VISUAL/ACTION]
She crosses the rug to the desk. She does not sit. She lays one palm flat on the leather of the chair-back. She holds it there for a count. Then she pulls the chair out, sits down, and rests both forearms on the desk.

She lifts the slim velvet pouch she has been carrying all morning from her trouser pocket. She tips her mother's gold pin into her palm — the pin Victoria handed her in EP49's bridal room, only now she carries it everywhere — and lays it on the desk in front of her. One small curve of gold on dark wood. She does not weep. She does not speak the moment out loud.

[KEY CAMERA]
- MCU Lena settling into the chair, both forearms on the desk
- ECU the gold pin on the wood, the necklace at her throat in the same frame
- CU her hand resting flat on the desk beside the pin
- Slow rack to the framed photo on the desk — Helena Sterling, mid-30s, laughing

[VO]
LENA (V.O.)
I had spent eleven years thinking of this chair as something I would have to earn. I had spent the last year thinking of it as a thing I had been asked to take. Sitting in it for the first time, I understood it was neither. It was just the room my father had worked in, and now I was going to work in it.

[VISUAL/ACTION]
She picks up the desk phone — old, corded, the same handset her father used. She dials Noah from memory, no scrolling.

[DIALOGUE]
LENA
(into the phone, quiet)
It's done. I'm in his chair. I'm not getting out of it. Come over when you can. Bring lunch.

(she hangs up)

She sets the phone in its cradle. She picks up the gold pin and slides it back into the velvet pouch. She slides the pouch into her trouser pocket. She turns her chair toward the window onto the garden and looks out at it for a long count.


## S#2. Sterling House — Father's Study · The Notification

[VISUAL/ACTION]
Forty minutes later. Lena is still in the chair. The leather binder of the Helena Sterling Foundation's pending grant list is open on the desk now. She has been making small careful notes in pencil in the margin. Her phone, face-down on the desk at her elbow, lights up. She turns it over.

[KEY CAMERA]
- MCU Lena at the desk, phone in hand, face still
- OTS Lena → phone screen
- ECU phone — a single push notification visible
- Wide of the study, the garden window behind her

[INSERT/CUTAWAY]
Phone screen, push notification from a national news app:

> EILEEN VOSS, 58 — DIED THIS MORNING IN CUSTODY AT WOMEN'S CORRECTIONAL ANNEX 3. PRELIMINARY CAUSE: CARDIAC EVENT. NO FOUL PLAY SUSPECTED.

[VO]
LENA (V.O.)
I had seen Eileen Voss face to face once, in a small room with a guard at the door, six weeks earlier. She had refused every visit after that one. The visits had been mine to request. The refusals had been hers. I had not gone again because she had not let me. I would have. The push notification on my phone at my father's desk was the way I was going to find out she was dead. I had decided that was the way she had wanted it, the moment she had refused the second visit.

[VISUAL/ACTION]
Lena sets the phone down face-up this time. She does not move it again. She looks once at the framed photo of her mother on the desk. She lays one hand flat next to the photo, fingers loose. She picks up the pencil. She finishes the note in the margin she had been making. She closes the binder.

[KEY CAMERA]
- ECU Lena's hand setting the phone down face-up
- CU her eyes — no movement, no narrowing, no widening
- MCU Lena finishing the margin note
- ECU the closed binder on the desk


## S#3. Sterling House — Father's Study · Continuous

[VISUAL/ACTION]
Lena does not leave her father's chair. She turns her chair toward the tall narrow window onto the garden. She lifts her own phone — the one face-up on the desk — and dials a number she has been keeping in her favorites for three weeks without using it.

[KEY CAMERA]
- MCU Lena at the desk, phone at her ear
- CU her hand on the desk, knuckles relaxed
- Slow rack to the framed photo on the desk — Helena Sterling, mid-30s, laughing
- INTERCUT — Mara's end, see below

[INTERCUT — MARA'S END]
A small, sun-bleached white-walled apartment somewhere on a Mediterranean coast. MARA in a thin cotton shirt and bare feet, kitchen counter cluttered with a kettle and a half-finished crossword. Her hair is shorter now, jaw-length, no styling. Her surgical scars are visible at her left ear and right wrist; she does nothing to hide them. She is wiping her hands on a dish towel when the phone rings. She sees the name on the screen. She does not pick up immediately. On the fourth ring she puts the towel down and answers.

[DIALOGUE]
MARA
Lena.

LENA
Eileen died this morning. Cardiac event, in custody, no foul play. I thought you should hear it from me before you saw it on a news site.

MARA
(after a long beat — she sits down on a wooden chair)
Thank you. For calling. I — I'm okay. I think I'm okay. Are you?

LENA
I think I am, yes. I read the last page of my mother's letter yesterday. She mentioned Eileen by name. She wasn't kind about herself about it. I wanted to tell you that too.

MARA
What did she say.

LENA
That she sent the money for eleven years and never asked where it went. That she should have looked at you once before she decided who you were. She asked me to do the looking instead.

MARA
(a small breath — not quite a laugh, not quite a sob)
That's more than I would have asked of her.

(beat)

Get married already, Lena. Stop calling me with deaths and start calling me with something good.

LENA
(soft — a real smile, alone in her office, no audience)
Working on it. Three weeks.

MARA
Send a real photo. I want to see the dress.

LENA
I will.

She does not say goodbye in any theatrical way. She says it like a person ending a phone call.

LENA
Take care of yourself. I'll write back this time.

[VISUAL/ACTION]
Lena lowers the phone. She sets it down on the desk beside her father's old corded handset. She reopens the foundation binder. She writes one short line at the bottom of the page she'd been working on: *Eileen Voss — buried Tuesday, family arrangement, no statement.* She closes the binder. She slides it into the desk drawer.


## S#4. Sterling House — Front Hall · Early Evening

[VISUAL/ACTION]
Lena comes down the front stairs in stocking feet, jacket off, sleeves rolled to her elbows for the first time today. NOAH is standing in the front hall holding two paper takeout bags and a single key on a ring she's never seen before. He's leaning against the newel post in his dark sweater and investigator coat. He has clearly been waiting at least ten minutes and not minded.

[KEY CAMERA]
- Lena descending the stairs, MCU on her bare-stocking feet
- MCU Noah at the newel post, key visible between his fingers
- Two-shot at the bottom of the stairs

[DIALOGUE]
NOAH
I have news. Good or bad — your call which one.

LENA
(taking the takeout bag from him, peeking inside)
You picked the soup place again.

NOAH
You said you liked it.

LENA
Tell me the news.

NOAH
(holding up the key)
The lake house is officially ours. Closing went through at four. Caretaker is meeting us up there Friday at noon if you want to drive up, otherwise we sign the rest of the paperwork in the city.

[END HOOK]
Lena takes the key from his palm. She closes her fingers around it. The metal is still warm from his hand. She looks at the key for one held beat — that exact house, the upstairs room where Mara confronted her at EP34, the lake she watched freeze over and thaw twice — then she looks up at Noah.

LENA
Friday. We drive up.

NOAH
I'll have the car loaded by ten.

Lena slides the key into the small inner pocket of her trouser waistband. She doesn't put it down anywhere it could be lost. She turns toward the kitchen with the soup bag in one hand. Hold on Noah, three steps behind her, watching the back of her head with the look of a man who has been preparing for a wedding for six months and only now believes it.

Hard Cut.

# SHE STOLE MY FACE — EP48

## S#1. Lake House — Exterior · Friday Afternoon

[VISUAL/ACTION]
The lake house sits on a low rise above clear water, a long covered porch wrapping its front, white clapboard freshly repainted. The boathouse below has new windows. The dock has new planks. Everything inside the property is the same shape Lena remembers from EP34 and everything is different in the small ways that matter — new locks on every door, a new rug in the front room, the upstairs guest room rebuilt entirely so it looks nothing like the room where Mara cornered her two years ago. LENA pulls up the drive in Noah's car, Noah at the wheel. She steps out before he has come around to her side.

A medium-sized brown rescue dog comes barreling out the side door of the house, slips on the porch boards, recovers, and launches herself at Lena's knees. The caretaker, MRS. HEINEY (mid-60s, denim shirt, no makeup), follows at a walk. The dog has a faded red collar and a tag that reads, in small block letters, PESTO.

[KEY CAMERA]
- Wide of the lake house from the drive, late afternoon light
- MCU Lena stepping out of the car, taking in the porch
- CU the dog (Pesto) bumping her shins
- Two-shot Lena and Noah, dog between them

[DIALOGUE]
LENA
(crouching, scratching Pesto behind one ear)
Hi. Hi. You are the most rude welcome committee in this county.

MRS. HEINEY
She came with the shelter recommendation you put on the file in March. She's been here a week getting used to the place. Hope that's still all right.

LENA
(looking up at Noah)
You moved a dog in without telling me.

NOAH
I told you I was talking to the shelter. I figured the surprise was the part you'd actually like. Her name was Pesto when they brought her in. I didn't change it because she answered to it.

LENA
(standing, dusting her knees)
Pesto stays. Pesto, you stay.

PESTO sits, more or less, then immediately fails the sit and rolls onto her back.


## S#2. Lake House — Upstairs Bedroom · Friday Evening

[VISUAL/ACTION]
Upstairs, the small bedroom Lena and Noah will share is warm with one yellow lamp and the last grey light over the lake through an open window. The wedding is in 48 hours. Lena's wedding dress hangs in the open closet under a long muslin garment bag — visible only as a column of ivory silk through the seam, the train pooled carefully on a clean white sheet on the closet floor. On a low wooden bench at the foot of the bed: two small velvet ring boxes, side by side, both still closed. Lena is in a soft cream sweater and pajama pants, her mother's necklace still at her throat. Noah is in a dark t-shirt and grey pants, barefoot.

This is the night before the rehearsal. They have agreed — privately, weeks ago — to do the real vows tonight, the two of them alone, before the public ceremony day after tomorrow.

[KEY CAMERA]
- Wide of the bedroom, lamp light + lake light
- MCU two-shot Lena and Noah sitting on the bench beside the ring boxes
- ECU the two velvet ring boxes
- INSERT — the closet seam showing the column of ivory silk

[DIALOGUE]
NOAH
You first, or me first.

LENA
Me. If I wait I'll edit it again.

NOAH
Go.


## S#3. Lake House — Upstairs Bedroom · The Private Vows

[VISUAL/ACTION]
Lena opens the box on her knee. Inside is a plain platinum band, slim, no engraving on the outside, one small line inscribed on the inner curve that we do not see this scene. She lifts it out and holds it between her thumb and forefinger. She does not look at Noah's face when she begins — she looks at her own hand. She finds his face by the second sentence.

[KEY CAMERA]
- MCU two-shot Lena and Noah on the bench
- ECU Lena's platinum band held between thumb and forefinger
- CU Lena's face, eyes lifting from the ring to Noah
- ECU Noah's hand offered, palm down, fingers loose

[DIALOGUE]
LENA
(quiet, looking at the ring, then at him)
For most of the time you'd known me I'd been trying to prove I exist. You showed up before I knew that was what I was doing, and you stayed inside the room until I stopped having to. That's what I want to promise back. I'll show up before you ask me to. I won't be careful with you the way I had to be careful with everyone else.

(beat — small, almost a smile)

I love you. I wanted to say it tonight before I had to say it in front of forty other people.

[VISUAL/ACTION]
She slides the ring onto his fourth finger. It fits. Noah looks at it for a second on his own hand. Then he opens the box on his own knee. Inside, a matching slim platinum band, slightly heavier. He lifts it out the same way she did.

NOAH
(low, steady, not theatrical)
I'd spent six years investigating people I didn't trust for a living before I ever sat across from you. The first time you told me what had happened to you, I didn't know yet that I would marry you. I knew I hadn't heard the truth in a room for a very long time and that I'd just heard it. That's not a feeling I'm willing to live without anymore. I'll tell you the truth in this house. When I can't carry something I'll say so, and I'll let you carry it back.

(beat)

I love you. I've been saying it to you in other words for two years. I'm glad I get to use the short version finally.

[VISUAL/ACTION]
He slides the matching band onto her fourth finger. It fits. Lena turns her hand once in the lamplight to look at it. The diamond at her throat catches a little of the same light. Neither of them speaks for a long beat.

LENA
(very quiet)
One kiss. We agreed.

NOAH
(half-smile)
One.

[KEY CAMERA]
- MCU two-shot pulling slightly closer on the bench
- ECU Lena's hand reaching first, fingers sliding up the inside of his wrist to his forearm
- CU his hand finding her jaw, thumb against the line of her cheekbone
- ECU their mouths almost touching, breath caught between them for a beat that holds
- ECU lips meeting — slow, open mouth, his lower lip catching her upper, her breath leaving once against his mouth
- MCU hold, foreheads still touching when they pull back, neither of them moving away

[VISUAL/ACTION]
They kiss. T2 — fully dressed, slow, real, and not chaste. Lena reaches first. Her hand comes up along the inside of his wrist and closes on his forearm, her thumb pressed to the cord of muscle there. His hand at her jaw is not tentative — his palm warm at her cheek, his thumb tracing once along the bone before his mouth opens against hers. The kiss is one held breath. She lets out a small sound against his mouth and he answers it in his throat, low, without breaking the kiss. The platinum band on her fourth finger presses cold against the skin of his forearm. When they pull back her forehead rests against his and neither of them moves, both of their hands still where they had landed. Noah's breath is uneven. So is hers.

The duvet at the foot of the bed has not been turned down. They both look at it once. They both look away again. The patience is the point.


## S#4. Lake House — Upstairs Bedroom → Window · Later That Night

[VISUAL/ACTION]
The rings stay on. The boxes go back on the bench, empty. Lena and Noah get into bed in the soft cream sweater and dark t-shirt they've been wearing — no undressing, no sex, the wedding is in 48 hours and they are choosing the patience. Lena pulls the duvet up to her shoulders. Noah switches off the yellow lamp. The room goes blue with lake light.

PESTO comes up the stairs uninvited, evaluates the situation, and curls up at the foot of the bed with the loud sigh of a dog who has decided she lives here now.

[KEY CAMERA]
- MCU Lena lying on her side, ring catching the lake light
- OTS Lena → Noah, his eyes still open
- Slow camera pan to the open window — first light over the lake won't come for hours yet, but the wind on the water is silver

[MONTAGE — quiet, four beats over slow lake-light]
1. The ivory silk dress in the closet, motionless
2. The two empty velvet ring boxes on the bench
3. Lena's hand on the pillow, platinum band on her fourth finger
4. The window — lake in the dark, one boat light far out

[DIALOGUE]
LENA
(into the dark, casual)
If I look nervous tomorrow at the rehearsal, kick me under the altar.

NOAH
Deal.

LENA
And don't let my Uncle Robert give a toast. He will. He'll try.

NOAH
I'll handle Uncle Robert. Go to sleep.

LENA
I'm going.

[END HOOK]
Lena reaches one arm out from under the duvet and finds Noah's hand on top of the covers. She doesn't lace fingers; she just rests the back of her hand against the back of his, both rings touching once with a small bright click of metal. Hold on the two rings, side by side in the half-dark, the lake light moving slowly across both bands. Outside the window, far across the water, a single boat light blinks twice and is still.

Hard Cut.

# SHE STOLE MY FACE — EP49

## S#1. Lake House — Bridal Room · Wedding Morning

[VISUAL/ACTION]
The smaller of the two upstairs bedrooms has been converted for the morning into a bridal room. Late spring sun slants through three open windows over the lake. There is no hairstylist, no makeup artist — Lena has refused both. Her one cousin, OLIVIA (early 20s, dark hair pinned simply, lavender dress), is fastening the back of the ivory silk gown for her. The gown is bare-shouldered with a long train pooled on a clean white sheet across the floor. Lena's hair is half-up with one fresh white peony pinned above her left ear. Mr. Bannon (today in a charcoal morning coat) is officiating the ceremony as a family attorney, not as clergy — a small private exception they made when Lena asked him weeks ago. A small wrapped box sits on the dresser, ribbon untied. Pesto is sleeping on the sheet at the foot of the train, snoring lightly.

[KEY CAMERA]
- Wide of the bridal room, three windows of lake light
- MCU Olivia at Lena's back, fastening the silk row of hooks
- CU the train pooled on the sheet, Pesto in frame
- ECU Lena's left hand, the platinum band from last night
- INSERT — the small wrapped box on the dresser, ribbon still tied

[DIALOGUE]
OLIVIA
Hold still one more second. There. You can breathe.

LENA
(quiet laugh)
That's a generous use of the word breathe.

OLIVIA
Mom is downstairs telling everyone where to sit. She's making people cry already. I told her to stop.

LENA
Let her. It's her one wedding to be at this year.

A small knock. VICTORIA appears in the doorway in a dove-grey dress, no pearls, the thin gold chain she wore in EP45 at her collar. She is here as a guest, not as a mother-in-law. She stays a half-step out of the doorway.

VICTORIA
I won't come in if you don't want me to.

LENA
Come in. It's all right.

VICTORIA
(stepping in, holding out a small flat velvet pouch)
This was your mother's. I found it last month in the safe at the house — Helena left it there the season before she got sick, and I never sent it back. It's a small pin. She wore it under her collar on her own wedding day. I thought you might want it on yours.

She opens the pouch and tips a small gold pin into Lena's palm — a simple curve of gold, no stones, the size of a thumbnail. Lena closes her hand over it for a second. She doesn't say anything dramatic. She just pins it carefully to the inside of the gown at her ribcage, where no one will see it but where she will know it is there.

LENA
Thank you. I mean that.

VICTORIA
I know you do. I'll be in the third row. I'm not giving any speeches.

[GRAPHIC/UI]
Lower-third chyron, off a single muted family TV downstairs (visible in background as Victoria leaves): no press. NO BROADCAST FEED. The TV is showing a baseball game with the sound off. That is the point.


## S#2. Lake House — Lakeside Aisle · The Ceremony Begins

[VISUAL/ACTION]
The ceremony is set on the lawn between the porch and the lake. Forty white wooden folding chairs in two arcs. A simple arch at the front of the aisle — bare wood, no flowers, the lake itself the backdrop. Family on one side, close friends on the other. Mr. Bannon stands under the arch with a single sheet of paper. Noah is already at the arch in a charcoal morning coat with no boutonniere; the bare lapel is intentional. Ethan sits in the fifth row on the family side, suit dark, no engagement band on his hand, no expression beyond the still attention of a man who has been told he is welcome and has decided to honor that by being quiet. Victoria is in the third row.

Pesto trots down the aisle ahead of the bridal party with no ribbon, no flower collar — just Pesto, off-leash, doing reconnaissance. The forty guests laugh, low and real, the kind of laugh you can hear at a small wedding. Olivia holds Lena's train as Lena walks down the grass aisle on the arm of her UNCLE ROBERT.

- MCU Noah at the arch, watching Lena walk
- OTS Lena → Noah down the length of the aisle
- ECU Pesto trotting through the rows
- CU Lena's face, calm, not stage-radiant, just present

[DIALOGUE]
MR. BANNON
(when Lena reaches the arch — short, natural, no clerical lift)
I've known this family forty years. Helena would have liked today. We're going to keep it short, the way she would have wanted. Lena, Noah — say what you came here to say.


## S#3. Lake House — Lakeside Arch · The Public Vows

[VISUAL/ACTION]
Lena hands her small bouquet — three white peonies tied with twine, no florist work — to Olivia behind her. She takes both of Noah's hands. The private vows from last night belong to the upstairs bedroom; these are the public ones, said in front of forty people who matter.

[KEY CAMERA]
- MCU altar — Lena and Noah facing each other under the arch
- OTS Noah → Lena's face
- OTS Lena → Noah's face

[DIALOGUE]
LENA
I asked you once, a long time ago, why you kept coming back when everyone else had decided I was someone they recognized but didn't trust. You told me you'd rather listen to a person tell the truth than listen to a roomful of people repeat a lie. I've thought about that sentence almost every week since. I'm marrying you in front of forty people today because I want them to hear me say out loud what I already say to you in the kitchen. You make it possible to put my own name down without flinching. I will spend the rest of my life making it that easy for you to put yours down.

I love you, Noah.

NOAH
(low, steady, the way he speaks when something matters)
The first photograph I ever took of you was a surveillance frame. I'm not proud of that and I won't pretend it didn't happen. I'm standing here today because the woman I was paid to watch turned out to be the only person in that room who was telling the truth, and I noticed, and I never went back to working that way again. You changed how I listen — at work, in the kitchen, at night when nothing's on fire. I'd like to be honest in your house for the rest of my life, and you in mine. That's the whole promise.

I love you, Lena.

[VISUAL/ACTION]
Mr. Bannon nods them through the ring exchange — a formality, because the rings are already on their hands from last night. They each slide their ring off, slide it back on. The diamond at Lena's throat catches the lake light through the arch.

(short)
You're married. Go on.

The forty guests stand. One small clap from Victoria, then the rest.


## S#4. Lake House — Lakeside Aisle · The Kiss

[VISUAL/ACTION]

## S#5. Lake House — Porch / Lawn · Reception Montage

[VISUAL/ACTION]
The forty chairs get folded away by cousins. A long wooden table appears on the porch with bread, cold meats, three salads, two bottles of champagne, and a single ridiculous three-tier cake that the cousin OLIVIA frosted herself badly. Music — a small string trio of music students from the city — sets up on the lawn. Late afternoon light deepens toward gold over the lake.

[MONTAGE — six beats, late afternoon to early evening]

1. CHAMPAGNE TOAST — Uncle Robert raises his glass on the porch. He says exactly two sentences ("To Lena and Noah. The next forty years.") and sits down. Noah catches Lena's eye across the porch — they share the small private laugh of two people who feared a longer speech.

2. LENA DANCES WITH MR. BANNON — On the lawn, music low, Lena in the ivory gown and Mr. Bannon in his charcoal morning coat. He has known her since she was eight. He leads her in a slow, unfancy box step. Neither of them is graceful and neither of them cares.

3. VICTORIA'S GIFT — Victoria approaches Lena under the porch light with a small wrapped box. Inside is a folded white linen handkerchief that was Helena's. Lena nods once, takes it, tucks it into Olivia's hand for safekeeping.

4. ETHAN'S TOAST — Ethan stands briefly at the edge of the porch with a glass. He does not raise his voice for the room. He raises it only for the porch — about twelve people hear him. "To Lena, who is finally being introduced as herself in a room I'm sitting in. And to Noah, who got it right the first time. That's all from me." He sits down.

5. POSTCARDS PINNED ON THE BOARD — On a side table inside the front room, a cork board has been hung up for the day. Every postcard Mara had sent over the last six months — seven of them now, from four different coastal towns. Olivia is adding the newest one, which had arrived two days ago — a small painted port scene, on the back: *Got a job teaching English starting next month. The kids will probably hate me. — M.*

6. PESTO AND THE BALLOON — One stray pink balloon escapes a cousin's child. Pesto chases it across the lawn, leaps, misses, leaps again, finally catches it in her mouth, pops it, and looks personally betrayed at the sound. Three guests laugh. The lake behind her goes orange.

[KEY CAMERA]
- For each montage beat — a single sustained shot, no fast cuts
- INSERT — the postcards on the cork board, Mara's handwriting close up
- Wide of Pesto on the lawn against the orange lake

[INTERCUT — Mara, briefly]
Mara at a small wooden table by an open window in her coastal apartment, evening light slanting in. She is reading something on her phone — the live photo feed Olivia has been sending her all afternoon. She is smiling, alone, with a glass of something pale in her hand. She does not look at the camera; the camera leaves quietly.


## S#6. Lake House — Candlelit Path to the Car · Late Evening

[VISUAL/ACTION]
The reception is winding down. Lena and Noah are leaving for the night — they will be back at the house tomorrow morning, but tonight they are driving to an inn on the other side of the lake. The cousins have lined the gravel path from the porch to the car with small glass jars holding tea lights — uneven spacing, slightly windblown, real. Pesto follows them halfway down the path, sits, watches them go without complaining.

[KEY CAMERA]
- Wide of the candlelit path, lake to the left
- MCU two-shot Lena and Noah walking
- CU Lena's hem on the gravel, train gathered up in one hand
- ECU Pesto sitting halfway down the path

[VISUAL/ACTION]
Halfway down the path, Noah turns to Lena, hooks one arm around her waist, and lifts her off the gravel for two paces — not a stage lift, an actual lift, the way someone picks up a person they love when they have eaten too much cake and want to be a little silly. Lena laughs — a real, surprised laugh — and grabs his shoulder to steady herself. He sets her down. They keep walking.

[DIALOGUE]
LENA
You're going to throw your back out.

NOAH
Worth it.

At the car, before they get in. He stops her with one hand at her elbow. He's not theatrical about it.

NOAH
Tomorrow we don't have to do anything.

LENA
(quiet, looking at him in the candlelight)
Tomorrow let's do nothing.

[END HOOK]
Noah opens the passenger door. Lena gathers the train carefully into the footwell. He closes the door. He walks around the back of the car. From the porch, far behind them, a faint string-trio note carries over the lawn — the music students playing one more song they hadn't been asked for. Pesto trots back up toward the porch and the music. Hold on the car taillights pulling out of the gravel drive, two red points, and behind them — through the back windshield — the lake house porch glowing yellow against the dark lake. Both rings catch a single instant of the porch light in Lena's hand on the dashboard as the car turns onto the road.

Hard Cut.

# SHE STOLE MY FACE — EP50

## S#1. Helena Sterling Foundation — Offices, Lower Manhattan · One Year Later · Late Afternoon

[VISUAL/ACTION]
A converted brick-and-window second-floor office, six small rooms off a central corridor, brass plate on the door reading HELENA STERLING FOUNDATION. The space is modest on purpose — three caseworkers, one part-time attorney, one front-desk volunteer. It is the kind of office where the kettle is always on. Late October light comes in low through the long front windows over Worth Street. The foundation has been operational for ten months. It has helped forty-one women rebuild their legal names. LENA — in dark slacks and a soft charcoal sweater, the mother's necklace at her throat, the platinum band on her left hand — sits in the smaller of two interview rooms across a low table from a woman named CLAIRE GERARDI.

CLAIRE is mid-30s, a thin scar at her hairline, a folder of court documents on her lap, hands that have not yet remembered how to be still. The folder is finished. Her case closed last week. This is the last conversation in the foundation's workflow — the one Lena always takes herself, no caseworker, just two women in a quiet room.

[KEY CAMERA]
- Wide of the small interview room, autumn light through the window
- MCU two-shot Lena and Claire across the low table
- CU Claire's hands on the folder, slowly relaxing
- ECU the brass plate on the door, the foundation's name visible

[DIALOGUE]
LENA
Your last name is on every document the city has now. Your bank, your lease, your insurance. The driver's license came in this morning — that was the slow one. You don't have to come back here for anything. If something else goes wrong in a year, you call me, not the front desk.

CLAIRE
(small, almost not a laugh)
You give your number to everyone you do this with?

LENA
Yes. I haven't regretted it. You're not the kind of person who is going to call me at midnight, Claire. I've gotten good at telling.

CLAIRE
What if I want to anyway. Once. Just to say it actually worked.

LENA
Then call. I'd take that one.

(beat — Claire breathes out, the long breath of someone setting something down for the first time in a long time)

CLAIRE
I keep waiting for someone to take it back. I keep checking my mail.

LENA
(quiet, more direct)
Eight years ago I was the woman in the chair you're sitting in, except there was no chair like it and no door like this one. The first morning I had my own name back on a wire transfer I went down to my bank and asked the teller to read it out loud. He thought I was crazy. I tipped him forty dollars. That feeling you're describing — checking the mail — that's the last thing it does before it stops. You'll know when it stops.

[FLASHBACK — 2 seconds, no dialogue]
A wet storefront window at night, the small jewelry-shop window from EP01 where Mara stood with the necklace pressed against the glass. Lena's reflection ghosted faintly in the same window. The image holds for one beat and dissolves.

[VISUAL/ACTION]
Claire breathes out once more — longer this time. The folder lowers another inch in her arms.

CLAIRE
(standing, lowering the folder all the way)
Thank you. I won't call at midnight.

LENA
(also standing, walking her to the door)
Take care of yourself. Eat something hot tonight.

[VISUAL/ACTION]
Claire goes out through the corridor. The front-desk volunteer hands her a small paper envelope on the way out — a copy of her own paperwork to keep. Claire pauses in the doorway to the street, looks at the brass plate one second, then steps down into late afternoon Worth Street. The door closes. Lena watches from the corridor for a beat. She does not stand there long. She walks back to her small office, sits down at the desk, opens her phone.

[INSERT/CUTAWAY]
Phone screen — Lena's text thread with Noah:

> LENA: Done for the day. You free?
> NOAH: Lake house. Bring takeout.
> LENA: Soup place?
> NOAH: Soup place.

[VISUAL/ACTION]
She closes the phone. She slides it into her pocket. She picks up her coat. She turns off the desk lamp. The small office goes blue with autumn evening.

[GRAPHIC/UI]
Lower-third caption, faintly visible on a muted radio at the front desk as Lena leaves: HELENA STERLING FOUNDATION — 41 NAMES RESTORED, ONE YEAR


## S#2. Lake House — Porch · Sunset, Same Evening

[VISUAL/ACTION]
The lake house porch. October sunset over still water. Long shadows. Two wide wooden chairs side by side facing the water — the chairs they bought in the spring, sun-faded already. A glass-topped low table between them with a bottle of pale wine, two glasses, a brown paper soup bag, and a folded cardigan Lena hasn't put on yet because she hasn't gotten cold. NOAH is in a dark sweater and jeans, sleeves pushed to his forearms, hair a little longer now than at the wedding. LENA is in the charcoal sweater she wore at the office, the mother's necklace still at her throat, the platinum band on her left hand. PESTO is asleep on the porch boards between the chairs, tail twitching at a dream.

A cork board has been moved out to the porch railing — it's still the same cork board from the wedding reception, only now it's almost full. Twelve postcards. Mara's handwriting, four different coastal towns, one printed photo of a small classroom of kids waving at the camera with Mara's hand-lettered caption underneath.

[KEY CAMERA]
- Wide of the porch from the lawn, lake light behind, two chairs in silhouette
- MCU two-shot Lena and Noah in the chairs
- ECU Pesto asleep on the boards
- INSERT — the cork board on the railing, twelve postcards

[VISUAL/ACTION]
Lena reaches across, takes the newest postcard off the board, holds it where Noah can see. She reads it out loud — not performed, the way you'd read a friend's note to someone in the same room as you.

[INSERT/CUTAWAY]
Postcard front: a painted port skyline, terracotta roofs against grey-blue sea. Back: Mara's handwriting, blue ballpoint, four lines.

> Three months into the new job teaching English. The kids think my accent is funny and they say so. The ocean here is louder than the last place — I think it's the rocks. Send me a real picture of the dog. — M.

[DIALOGUE]
LENA
(reading the last line, looking up at Noah)
She wants a picture of the dog.

NOAH
(half-smile)
Pesto has not been photogenic at any point this year. We're going to disappoint her.

LENA
We're going to send it anyway.

NOAH
We are.

[VISUAL/ACTION]
Noah picks up his phone. He aims it at Pesto, who is at this exact moment sleeping with her tongue half out and one eye not entirely closed. He takes the photo. He shows Lena the screen. They both laugh — quiet, real, the laugh of two people on a porch at sunset.

[KEY CAMERA]
- CU Noah's phone, photo of Pesto, ridiculous
- MCU two-shot Lena and Noah, laughing
- ECU Lena's hand reaching for the wine glass, both rings catching the sunset

[VISUAL/ACTION]
Noah pours her a glass of the pale wine. He hands it to her. She takes it. They settle back into the chairs. Pesto registers the laughter and lifts her head once, decides nothing has actually happened, drops her head again.

[MONTAGE — five quiet beats, sunset deepening]

1. The lake — flat silver going gold
2. A single late goose flying low across the water
3. The brass plate on the lake house front door — *LENA AND NOAH KEENE*
4. The cork board on the porch railing, twelve postcards, October wind moving one of them once
5. The two wedding bands on the armrest of Lena's chair, side by side with her hand and his

[DIALOGUE]
LENA
(watching the lake, casual, not punchline-shaped)
I'd been waiting to feel like I'd gotten away with something. It hadn't gone away. I'm done waiting.

NOAH
(after a beat, looking at her sideways, not at the lake)
I think that's just being happy. Took me about a year to figure that out too. You stop checking over your shoulder eventually. Or you don't, and you get used to checking.

LENA
(small)
That's a generous reframe.

NOAH
I work in a generous field now.

[VISUAL/ACTION]
She turns her head to look at him. He's already looking at her. He stands up out of his chair without warning, leans down, and lifts her off her chair — one arm under her knees, one at her back, the way he had at the candlelit path on the wedding night, only ordinary now, the way you lift a person you live with. She laughs once — surprised, real — and grabs his shoulder. He carries her three paces to the porch railing. He sets her down with her back against the wood. The cork board of postcards is a foot to her right. The lake light is on her face.

He kisses her once. Slow, open, no choreography. Her hand finds the front of his sweater and closes there. His hand is at her jaw. The diamond at her throat catches the last bar of sun. Pesto opens one eye on the porch boards, watches them for two seconds, and goes back to sleep, having decided this is what these two do now.

When he pulls back his forehead rests against hers for a beat.

[DIALOGUE]
NOAH
(quiet, against her hair)
Inside.

LENA
(quiet)
Inside.

[VISUAL/ACTION]
He does not let go of her. He picks up the wine bottle with one hand, leaves the glasses on the table, and walks her toward the porch door, one arm still at her waist. The screen door swings open. Pesto lifts her head, considers her options, decides the porch is fine, and stays. The screen door swings closed behind them.

[KEY CAMERA]
- MCU Noah lifting Lena out of the chair, her arm around his shoulder
- ECU her hand closing on the front of his sweater
- CU the kiss against the porch railing, her hand at his chest, his hand at her jaw
- ECU the cork board on the railing a foot from her shoulder — twelve postcards, Mara's handwriting
- Wide pull-back — the porch from the lawn, the two of them moving through the screen door, the empty chairs and the wine glasses on the table behind them
- CU Pesto on the porch boards, one eye open, then closed
- Wide of the lake at full sunset — copper to blue, no foreground

[VISUAL/ACTION]
The screen door has closed. The porch goes quiet. The two wide wooden chairs sit empty side by side, the wine glasses on the table between them catching the last bar of light. The cork board moves once in the October wind, one postcard lifting and settling. Pesto stays where she is. Inside the lake house, beyond the screen door, a single lamp comes on in the front room, warm yellow against the dark wood. We do not follow.

The lake light goes orange-red one last time and fades. Pesto does not move. The porch goes from gold to dim.

Slow fade.

Fade Out.
